TWELFTH NATIONAL CONVEN- TION OF THE AMERICAN LEGION 

GREETINGS AND APPRECIATION HE 1930 National Convention Corporation of the American Legion extends to you a most cordial and sincere welcome to Boston, Massachusetts, and New England on this, the occasion of the Twelfth National Convention of the American Legion and its allied organizations, the American Legion Auxiliary, Forty an' Eight Society, and Eight an' Forty Society.

We hope that this Guidebook and the General Convention Information insert will be most helpful to you, making more convenient your traveling about to see our historic places. 

We take this opportunity to express our appreciation of the splendid coöperation and generous spirit of Ginn and Company as manifested, in part, by its contribution of this fine Guidebook, including this insert of General Convention Information especially prepared for the Official 

Delegations of our Conventions. 

The great Athenæum Press of Ginn and Company in Cambridge is one of the oldest establishments of the kind in America and for more than sixty years has produced a vast number of the textbooks which have been used in the schools of this country. 

JOHN D. CROWLEY Executive Secretary CARROLL J. SWAN President

HARVARD UNIVERSITY LIBRARY GENERAL CONVENTION INFORMATION HEADQUARTERS Boston Convention Committees Headquarters: First Corps of Cadets Armory, 

Columbus Avenue (opposite Hotel Statler). All local convention committees will have headquarters here to handle all convention arrangements within their scope.

Legion National Headquarters Divisions: Horticultural Hall, Huntington Avenue at Massachusetts Avenue. 

Auxiliary National Headquarters: Symphony Hall, Huntington Avenue at Massachusetts Avenue. 

Forty and Eight National Headquarters: Horticultural Hall and Hotel Touraine. 

Eight and Forty National Headquarters: Hotel Somerset, Commonwealth Avenue. 

CONVENTION HALLS American Legion: Boston Arena, St. Botolph Street at Massachusetts Avenue. 

American Legion Auxiliary: Symphony Hall. 

Forty and Eight Society: Horticultural Hall. 

Eight and Forty Society: Horticultural Hall. 

REGISTRATION AND INFORMATION 

Main headquarters for both Registration and Information are located in the Cadet Armory (Park Square section of city). Additional information and registration booths will be found at railroad stations, Boston Common, convention halls, official parking areas, and principal hotels. 

Twenty-four-hour service maintained at main booths; 8 A. M. to midnight at others. Badges, books of tickets, and programs distributed at main booths to registrants only.

HOUSING 

All assignments of housing made at Housing Committee Headquarters, 90 Arlington Street (Hotel Statler Building). (See Convention Housing 

Directory, p. 12.)

TELEPHONE SERVICE 

The New England Telephone and Telegraph Company has established a special Convention Exchange for official convention business, connecting all convention halls, headquarters, committee chairmen, committee rooms, and departments headquarters. This special exchange number is Back Bay 6600. 2

GENERAL CONVENTION INFORMATION POSTAL SERVICE 

The Postmaster at Boston has established a special American Legion 

National Convention Post Office at the Convention Committees Head quarters in the First Corps of Cadets Armory. All incoming mail addressed to anyone in care of this special Post Office may be obtained there from 8:30 A.M. to 7 P.M. 

RAILROAD TICKET VALIDATION 

Agents of all initial lines at Boston will be stationed at the Cadet Armory to validate special-rate railroad tickets, as required to obtain benefit of special convention rates. 

AUTO PARKING SPACES 

Several parking spaces have been provided, some free to convention registrants and others at a nominal parking fee. Special parking space is reserved for busses transporting Posts and Units who are to participate in the Convention Parade, October 7, at the Technology grounds, across the Charles River in Cambridge, near the Harvard Bridge. Camps for automobile parties who are registered are available free at Pine Banks Park, near the Malden-Melrose line, about eight miles from the center of Boston. Street car and bus service to Boston is readily available from all parking spaces.

CHURCH SERVICES 

Patriotic services and sermons in all churches in Greater Boston, Sunday, October 5, with main services in Holy Cross Cathedral (Catholic), Trinity Church (Episcopal), Tremont Temple (Baptist), Temple Israel (Jewish), and First Church of Christ Scientist. 

CATHOLIC CHURCHES 

Masses at 7, 8, 9, 10, and 11 A.M.

MEDICAL AID STATIONS 

Medical aid stations are established at all Convention Halls, Cadet 

Armory, Boston Common, Headquarters Hotels, United States Veterans' 

Bureau (600 Washington Street), and South Armory (Irvington Street), with physicians and nurses in constant attendance. Parade Day Stations will be maintained along the entire route of the parade. (See list of latrines and first-aid stations, p. 14.)

POLICING ARRANGEMENTS 

Convention policing arrangements headquarters are at the South Armory (Irvington Street) in liaison with Boston Police Department and State Patrol headquarters. These headquarters will be in direct contact with all official police units throughout the entire state. 

ADMITTANCE TO CONVENTION SESSIONS 

All Legion sessions will be held at the Arena. Delegates and Alternates only will be admitted to the floor area and must show their credential cards. Admission to reserved sections will be by Reserved Seat 

Ticket only. Main entrance only to be used. Wearers of Convention

Badge given preference to unreserved sections until formal opening of convention sessions. General public admitted thereafter if space permits. 

Convention sessions of American Legion Auxiliary, Forty and Eight Society, and Eight and Forty Society limited to members of said organizations, who must present membership cards as well as Convention Badge, if requested.

GENERAL CONVENTION INFORMATION HISTORIC TOURS 

General information regarding trips to the numerous historic places and shrines for which Boston and suburbs are famous may be obtained at the Historic Tour Section in the Cadet Armory. Walking tours have been arranged, with guides available at reasonable hours. Special arrangements have been made with selected agencies to secure special rates to all sections of New England. Special pamphlets regarding these trips are available at the Cadet Armory. Information on Post-Convention 

Tours available at that desk in the Armory. (See detailed tours listed herein, pp. 15-24.) Terminal: Boston Common at Charles Street Mall.

CONTESTS 

Detailed information regarding all contests may be obtained at Contests Committee Desk in the Cadet Armory. All organizations and individuals to take part must register there. It is important to keep in close touch with the committee in case of change of plans. 

LOCAL TRANSPORTATION 

Detailed information regarding all forms of local transportation may be obtained at all Information Booths and at Local Transportation Desk in the Cadet Armory. Organizations desiring special arrangements for group transportation should give adequate notice to Local Transportation Desk. Information concerning subway, elevated, street car, bus, taxi, local railroad, and steamboat service is readily available upon request. (See spot map herein for street railway service in convention area.)

REUNIONS 

Arrangements for reunions may be made through Reunions Committee at its desk in the Cadet Armory. Desk space for unit registration may be obtained in this section of Cadet Armory. Banquets should be arranged through this committee in liaison with Housing Committee. (See list herein, p. 24.)

CONVENTION COMMITTEE MEETINGS 

Committees of the Legion National Convention will meet at the State House (Beacon Hill), with a few exceptions, these will meet at Horticultural Hall and John Hancock Hall. Auxiliary Committees meet in 

Symphony Hall; Forty and Eight and Eight and Forty in Horticultural Hall. All may be reached on the telephone through Convention 

Telephone Exchange. 

GOLF 

Several golf clubs in the immediate vicinity offer their facilities to 

National Officers and Registrants. Tournaments have been arranged, with plenty of fine prizes.

All information available at Entertainment 

Desk, Cadet Armory.

6 

GENERAL CONVENTION INFORMATION PARADES 

There will be three parades during the Convention. Float Pageant 

Parade, Sunday afternoon; Forty and Eight Parade, Monday evening;

Legion Convention Parade (28 miles long), Tuesday. The same route, with elimination of business section for Sunday and Monday parades, will be followed, utilizing all grandstands for three parades. Formation area, west of Public Gardens to Massachusetts Avenue and between Boylston 

Street and Charles River Embankment. (See Parade details, pp. 26-30.)

PARADE REVIEWING STANDS The Official Reviewing Stand is located on the Lafayette Mall of Boston Common (Tremont Street) from Winter Street to Avery Street. 

Other stands will be located on Beacon Street, near Charles Street, Statler Square, Stuart Street, and Columbus Avenue. Seats for all stands except the Official Reviewing Stand are on sale at Grand Stand 

Desk in the Cadet Armory. Gold Star Group and Disabled Veterans will be provided for in Official Reviewing Stand as guests of the Convention Committee.

TRAFFIC REGULATION 

Convention area is divided into zones, in which traffic will be restricted. 

General traffic will be excluded from entire convention area Tuesday, October 7, during the parade. Vehicles from the west, north, and east will be diverted at Cambridge Esplanade and Fenway. Autoists should park their cars in outlying sections, readily available by local transportation, preferably in the parking spaces indicated. Press cars and emergency vehicles may obtain necessary "police passes at Police Arrangements Headquarters, South Armory, Irvington Street. (Obtain copy of traffic rules at Policing Desk in Armory.)

RADIO BROADCASTING 

Arrangements have been made for national broadcasting of all major events of the convention, and everyone is urged to coöperate with radio officials and press representatives in carrying out their assignments. 

Detailed information regarding same may be obtained at Publicity Desks, Horticultural Hall and Cadet Armory. 

SPECIFIC INFORMATION 

Every local committee has a desk at the Cadet Armory where specific information regarding all convention arrangements may be obtained. 

The National Headquarters Divisions are located at Horticultural Hall. 

Auxiliary Committees are at Symphony Hall. Forty and Eight Committees are at Hotel Touraine. Eight and Forty Committees are at Hotel Somerset.

NATIONAL OFFICERS OFFICIALS' IDENTIFICATION 

Members of the several convention committees will be designated by uniform arm brassards showing the name of particular committee. Various colors will be used to distinguish committees. Policing Coöperation 

Committee will wear red and white chapeaus, with red and white bras sards marked CO-OP. Officers of the Corporation will have brassards marked OFFICIAL. Automobiles of officials, national officers, and distinguished guests will have windshield stickers with Legion emblem marked OFFICIAL. Press representatives will have red badges marked PRESS. 

Convention Official Vendors will have badges plainly marked with Legion emblem and numbered for personal identification.

HISTORIC TOURS COMMITTEE 

15 Headquarters of this committee are at the Cadet Armory. All information booths are equipped to help Legionnaires who wish to see historic Boston and vicinity. Persons desiring information regarding places of interest and motor routes or established touring routes in other New England states should call at the various state headquarters. 

Busses, following already established or specially arranged sight-seeing routes in and about Boston, authorized by the Legion Convention Committee carry banner or placards announcing that fact. In addition there are many private companies operating busses and furnishing guides. 

At various central places of interest volunteers will be stationed to give information. There are no authorized Legion guides in Boston proper, but Boy Scout pilots will be found at the Army and Navy Monument on the Common. Members of the Auxiliary will find guides of their own organization who will conduct them about the city. 

The City of Boston and the Massachusetts Tercentenary Commission have placed markers at interesting points, and each old-time street will be found marked with names by which it has been known since first used. 

Throughout the city will be found tablets marking historic spots. 

The Boston Elevated Railroad reaches most places of local historic interest (see map).

The Boston and Maine and the New York, New Haven and Hartford railways have arranged special trains to Plymouth, Lexington, and Concord, and frequent train service is available for those who wish to spend a few hours at the nearby historic towns of Marblehead and Salem, or in slightly longer trips to Newburyport, Portsmouth, Providence, and New Bedford.

A FEW POINTS OF INTEREST AND HOW TO REACH THEM 

Old State House, Old South Church, Birthplace of Benjamin Franklin: take any street leading east from the Common and Tremont Street, proceed one block east to Washington Street, then turn north. Frank- 

Îin's birthplace is near the corner of Milk Street (see Walk No. 1). Old 

State House is two blocks beyond Old South Church. 

Boston Massacre, in front of Old State House, State Street. 

Faneuil Hall, one block north of State Street and east of Dock Square. 

Charlestown Navy Yard, Bunker Hill: take Boston Elevated Railway for City Square or Thompson Square station. 

Dorchester Heights: take Elevated to Andrew Square or take City 

Point car.

Eliot Square, Roxbury: take Elevated to Dudley Street and walk westerly to church on hill. Visit the old house at head of square on right. 

The present church is on site of church in which the famous John Eliot, Apostle to the Indians," preached. ९९ Art Museum, Huntington Avenue: cars pass the building, which is not far from the Arena and Symphony Hall.

Public Library, Copley Square. 

Harvard Square, Cambridge, and Harvard University: take trains in tunnel at Park Street, northeast corner of Common. 

Boston College: take Lake Street cars. 

Wellesley College: take Boston and Albany Railway at Trinity Court or South Station for Wellesley. From Wellesley station take bus or walk.

Also can be reached by street cars via Newton.

WALKS ABOUT OLD BOSTON 

Walk No. 1, not including stops at places of interest, may be made in less than one hour. To continue this walk over Walk No. 2 will take half as long again. Four hours will cover the entire course of both walks and allow for stopping at interesting places. 

It was the old North End of Boston which gave to the town its reputation of crooked streets and by-ways. Careful attention should be paid to street signs.

Walk No. 1 (Page references are to Bacon's Guide Book to Boston) 

Start from Blaxton Memorial, Beacon Street, site of cottage of first inhabitant of Boston; this is on edge of Common, between Charles 

Street and the State House (p. 74).

Boston Common (p. 32) is bounded on the north by Beacon Street. Just west of the State House is the site of the famous Hancock Mansion, home of Governor John Hancock, a signer of the Declaration of Independence. 

State House (enter at end of walk, which returns to this point).

Opposite State House is Robert Gould Shaw Memorial. 

Proceed east down Beacon Street to Tremont to King's Chapel and ancient burial ground (p. 26). Enter King's Chapel. The eastern end stands on site of first public school, 1635 (p. 51). 

School Street, continuation of Beacon. 

City Hall (p. 51). "Old Corner Bookstore," 1712 (p. 52). Site of home of Anne Hutchinson, banished 1638 (p. 53).

Opposite is site of last home of Governor John Winthrop, first governor 

Diagonally opposite is Old South Church (p. 53). Enter. Nearly opposite the church was the Old Province House, home of colonial governors after 1715 (p. 55). 

Milk Street, No. 17, Benjamin Franklin's birthplace.

Pass down Milk Street to open space where new Post Office is being erected on site of the old one. To the east were flats, and at the northeastern corner was a channel sufficient for small boats. One block south of Milk Street, on Devonshire Street, was site of first Catholic Cathedral in New England, 1803 (p. 56).

Army Base and Castle Island vantage points for observing tribute to 

Frigate Constitution, Old Ironsides in President Roads, Boston Harbor and nightly display of searchlights from U.S. warships. For Army Base, Dorchester Tunnel to South Sta., then Summer & D St. car to Army Base.

*Free parking for registered Legionnaires.

Turn north on Devonshire Street, pass Spring Lane, where once was a flowing spring, with Shawmut Bank Building on right at corner of 

Water Street (p. 57). 

Old State House, in middle of and at head of State Street. Enter. 

(Pp. 4, 8.) This was center of old Boston. To the north was the "North 

End," to the south the "South End." The old town extended from about 

School Street northerly and easterly to the water. 

27 State Street, site of First Church in Boston, 1632. 

Site of Boston Massacre, marked by blocks in pavement opposite 

Merchants National Bank. 

Down State Street to Custom House, passing No. 53, Exchange Build ing, site of Governor Winthrop's first residence (p. 11). The Custom 

House was once at head of the wharf at foot of State Street. 

Turn left on Commercial Street to Faneuil Hall (p. 12). Enter. 

Pass east out by Dock Square, turning north on Union Street, in which, at No. 16, was residence of William Dawes, who with Revere warned the patriots on the eve of the battle of Lexington. He was an ancestor of 

General Dawes, former Vice President.

Return to Dock Square and Adams Square, and pass northerly up Cornhill to Scollay Square. In this street was the Franklin printing office, where Benjamin learned his trade, and which was a principal rendezvous of the members of the Boston Tea Party (p. 17).

Cross Scollay Square and ascend by Pemberton Square to Court 

House. Pass through this, emerging at Ashburton Place, facing State 

House. Approach this by Beacon Monument and go under archway to 

Mt. Vernon Street.

Take doors on left and visit State House. The original Charter, granted in 1628, is to be seen on fourth floor, also original manuscript of Gover nor Bradford's own History of Plymouth Colony in the State Library. 

Walk No. 2 Proceed to Faneuil Hall by Walk No. 1 or directly. After visiting 

Faneuil Hall, turn on Union Street (p. 58), which is entered from Dock 

Square, and by North Street proceed to North Square (p. 60). 

Visit Paul Revere House, where he lived in 1775. Near by is site of 

Old North Church (p. 61). In olden times many fine residences were grouped in this vicinity (p. 62).

From North Square turn left into Prince Street, cross Hanover Street (p. 63), and on reaching Salem Street turn right and go to Christ Church. 

This is the oldest church edifice now standing in Boston; from its steeple were hung the lanterns Revere watched for on the eve of April 19, 1775. 

Enter. 

Turn left on Hull Street, opposite church, and proceed to Copps Hill Burial Ground (p. 65). Here are many old and quaint stones, and here a 

British battery fired on the patriot works at Bunker Hill. Near here, on the water front, is the only part of the original water front of Boston. A good view of the old harbor, also of Charlestown and many interesting points, may be had here.

Return either the way the hill was reached and by way of Hanover 

Street and Washington Street to Scollay Square (see Walk No. 1) or descend to Commercial Street on the water front. A turn to the left will take one to the North Station, or by turning to the right and following Atlantic Avenue along the water front one may reach the South Station at the foot of Summer Street in about twenty minutes. Near the foot of Congress Street, just before reaching the South Station, may be seen the tablet marking the wharf where the Boston Tea Party took place. 

LEGION AUTHORIZED BUS TOURS 

These special tours have been laid out to cover different routes to Concord and short trips into the environs of Boston. 

The transportation company carrying out these trips will have comfortable busses, which will leave the terminal as frequently as possible. 

Special low rates have been arranged. These tours are estimated to take from one to three hours, according to circumstances. 

Itineraries can be obtained at Headquarters of the Historic Tours 

Committee at Cadet Armory. 

TOUR TO LEXINGTON AND CONCORD 

(See page 134.) Information regarding special itineraries of this tour via Cambridge or via Roxbury and Brookline may be obtained from the 

Historic Tours Committee. 

The most direct route is by way of Harvard Square and Massachusetts Avenue. Tour may be made by bus and car lines, or direct by rail from 

North Station. Local committees at Cambridge Common, Arlington,

Lexington, and Concord will furnish information regarding historic places in their communities. 

A trip via Roxbury, Brookline, Wellesley, Weston, to Concord, and return via Lexington and Cambridge, will be found of great interest. 

Information may be obtained from the Historic Tours Committee. 

Information regarding short trips to Park System, educational institutions, South Shore, and to various places of historic interest within an hour's drive from Boston may be obtained from the Historic Tours Committee, Cadet Armory.

NORTH SHORE TOUR 

(See map, page 140)

This route is arranged for individual cars and will be followed to certain points by busses. Salem and Marblehead can be reached by regular busses and by railway from North Station.

A delightful tour leads from Boston along the North Shore, to the famous old shipping town of Newburyport. It is possible to return direct to Boston by the Newburyport Turnpike, making a circuit of 98.5 miles, or to continue inland by way of Lawrence, Andover, and Medford, cover-

ing 115.4 miles in all. Lowell, one of the great manufacturing cities of 

New England, is a few miles from Lawrence. 

Proceed to Revere Beach by one of the marked routes. This attractive beach was the first publicly owned seaside resort in the country. To the south of Revere Beach are the harbor fortifications of Fort Banks and Fort Heath, containing some of the largest mortars on the Atlantic Coast. The peninsula of Nahant is seen on the right; here was the summer home of Senator Henry Cabot Lodge, and here Longfellow, Whittier, N. P. Willis, Prescott, and Agassiz found inspiration.

Lynn (4 m.), older than Boston, settled in 1629. The first iron casting made in America, a kettle, is in the Public Library. Lynn leads in manufacture of women's shoes, cut stock, and other shoe findings. Here is one of the largest plants of the General Electric Company. Leave by Highway 129, the attractive Lynn Shore Drive, along King's Beach, following the road of 1629. 

Swampscott (1.8 m.), a residential suburb of Lynn. Cannon, Monument Square, captured by Swampscott men from a British frigate in the War of 1812. First tannery in the United States, established 1629. The "Swampscott Dory" used by deep-sea fishermen tells its own story. 

Marblehead (4.7 m.), favored by wealthy summer residents and yachtsmen, savors of old fishing and privateering days. Aërodrome of the Curtiss Aëroplane Company is located here. In the Town Hali hangs the famous painting, "The Spirit of '76," by Willard. The Lee mansion was designed by Bulfinch; here were entertained Washington and 

Lafayette. In State Street is the mansion of Colonel John Glover, whose regiment, consisting of nine hundred Marblehead seamen, made possible the withdrawal of the Continental forces from Long Island, 1776, and saved the day at Trenton by ferrying Washington's army across the Delaware. More than half the Constitution's crew were Marblehead men. 

Lincoln's first call for troops, 1861, reached the town late at night, but the first company to report in Boston the next morning was from Marblehead,

Salem (32 m.), after Plymouth the oldest permanent settlement in 

New England, settled in 1626 by Roger Conant, whose statue faces the common. Elias H. Derby in 1784 started the first American trade direct with Russia. In 1788 his ship, Atlantic, first displayed the American flag in leading Asiatic ports. At the beginning of the nineteenth century 

Joseph Peabody, another sea magnate, owned eighty-three ships and employed seven thousand seamen. Cargoes frequently realized 800 per cent profit. When the Boston Port Bill closed the port of Boston, Salem became the seat of government. Here the first Provincial Congress of Massachusetts met, and on February 26, 1775, at the North Bridge, the first armed resistance was offered to royal troops. (Consult local guide-book.) 

The name of Salem is associated with the witchcraft delusion of 1692; the earliest instances of witchcraft in New England occurred elsewhere. 

Associated with the name of Nathaniel Hawthorne, born in 1804, are his birthplace at 27 Union Street, the Custom House, and the House of 

Seven Gables, built in 1669, at 59 Turner Street; the Old Grimshawe

House, 53 Charter Street, was the scene of two of his stories, "Dr. 

Grimshawe's Secret" and "The Dolliver Romance."

The Peabody Museum, Essex Street, contains models and pictures of ships which brought Salem her prosperity, and other valuable collections. The Essex Institute, near by, contains a very large collection of antiquarian interest. 

Leave Salem by Highway 127. Magnificent estates border the wooded, winding road, which often runs close to the sea. 

Beverly (1.8 m.) contains many old houses. Here was the "Summer

White House" of President Taft, since cut in two, floated across the bay, and set up in Marblehead.

Pride's Crossing (3.7 m.), notable for fine estates. The late Senator 

Beveridge lived here.

Beverly Farms (.6 m.). The route skirts the estate formerly occupied by Philander C. Knox, now occupied by the Italian Ambassador, and the estates of John D. Rockefeller, Jr., and Justice Oliver Wendell

Holmes of the Supreme Court. Just beyond the station the route passes the old home of the latter's father, Dr. Oliver Wendell Holmes, the famous author.

Manchester-by-the-Sea, settled in 1626. The route passes the Essex 

Country Club, one of the best known on the coast, and the splendid 

Georgian house of the late T. Jefferson Coolidge, Jr. From Manchester a shore road leads by way of Raymond Street, on the right, to Magnolia (3.5 m.), "the Newport of Massachusetts." Here was the studio of the famous artist William Hunt Morris, and not far off shore is Norman's 

Woe, the treacherous reef of Longfellow's "Wreck of the Hesperus."

A mile and a half across the water is Eastern Point, the outer arm of 

Gloucester Harbor. To the left are the home of John Hays Hammond and the laboratory of his son, inventor of the wireless-controlled boat and torpedo and the thermos incendiary bomb. 

Gloucester (6.4 m.), founded as a fishing station, 1623, and today the greatest salt-fish port in the world. Cape Ann was named in honor of 

Ann of Denmark, mother of Charles I. The schooner rig originated in Gloucester about 1714. In 1775 Gloucester was second to Marblehead as a fishing port. About 1850 the fisheries began to revive. Between 

1830 and 1907 over five thousand lives and seven hundred vessels were lost from the Gloucester fishing fleet. The Gorton Pew Company and other fisheries are glad to show visitors over their plants. On Middle 

Street are at least ten houses built before the Revolutionary War; No. 47, unchanged both inside and outside since that day, contains one of the finest colonial interiors in Massachusetts. Leave Gloucester by Highway 121.

Essex (6.4 m.), an attractive village surrounded by a number of beautiful estates. Fishing craft are built here for Gloucester and Boston. 

In the meeting-house hangs a bell cast by Paul Revere. John Wise, pastor here in colonial times, was perhaps the first to enunciate the doctrine that taxation without representation is tyranny. Essex was the birthplace of Rufus Choate, one of the greatest of American lawyers.

Ipswich (4.9 m.), settled 1633, still retains its old New England atmosphere and boasts several fine colonial houses. The oldest building, and a fine example of the early house, is the Whipple House, built in 1650. It stands opposite the Railroad Station. The "hall," a combination kitchen and living-room, furnished in old-time fashion, is particularly interesting. The Ross Tavern, built in 1734, stands under a great elm on South Main Street. The Swasey House, where Washington stopped, is on County Street. At the end of the road, on a high hill, is the home of R. T. Crane, Jr., of Chicago.

On leaving Ipswich rejoin Highway 1A and pass through the old towns of Rowley and Newbury. Many old houses are passed some built before 1671.

Newburyport (11.5 m.) was formerly part of Newbury. The development of shipbuilding and of the East India trade brought the "wealth of the Indies" to Newburyport. During the Revolution numerous clippers were transformed into privateers, twenty-four being fitted out in this port, which captured over two hundred and twenty-five prizes and over twenty-two hundred men. In 1805 Newburyport's custom receipts were the third largest in any port in New England. Famous clipper ships were built and owned in Newburyport, among them the Dreadnaught, which crossed in 1860 from Sandy Hook to Queenstown in nine days and thirteen hours.

The finest houses of Newburyport are on High Street, among them that of "Lord" Timothy Dexter. The public library, on State Street, built in 1771, occupies the former residence of Nathaniel Tracy, the wealthiest merchant of the time. Some rooms are preserved as they were when Washington stayed there in 1789, and Lafayette in 1824. Opposite the famous old Wolfe Tavern, State Street, is the Dalton Club, former residence of Tristram Dalton, merchant prince and senator. Among onetime residents of Newburyport were George Whitefield, famous revivalist; 

William Lloyd Garrison, the abolitionist; and John Greenleaf Whittier, the poet.

From Newburyport it is possible to return to Boston by Newburyport Turnpike (35.7 m.), a straight road. At Topsfield, near Fair Grounds, a detour (right) can be made to the village and the Parson Capen house can be visited. This house, of the witchcraft period, has been restored. 

Just before reaching Hathorne Hill, on which is the Insane Hospital, after crossing railway bridge in Danvers (next town to Topsfield), is the birthplace of General Israel Putnam, famous soldier of Colonial and Revolutionary wars. The cross road (left) here leads to Salem through 

Danvers by some interesting old houses. If on the Salem road, at Summer Street, turn left beyond railroad bridge, pass the ancient Wadsworth cemetery and the James Putnam house, now an inn. The well immediately beyond marks the homestead of John Putnam, 1641. Seventy-five men bearing his name responded to the Lexington alarm April 19, 1775.

In the grove beyond is Oak Knoll, long the home of John Greenleaf Whittier. Turn left at St. John's Preparatory School and circle back to the main road.

If the return is made by way of Salem, take Danversport road from Danvers Square. Just beyond Railway Station may be seen the farm of 

Governor John Endicott, with the pear tree planted by him nearly three hundred years ago. This route leads across the North Bridge in Salem, the scene of Leslie's retreat February 26, 1775, when a British force which had landed at Marblehead and marched to Salem to destroy munitions was forced to abandon their objective. 

Return to Boston may be made from Salem by a shorter route than followed on way out. Follow North Street into Summer Street, turn right on Chestnut Street, a fine street with old-time substantial houses. 

The Pickering house, a fine example of the early colonial period, is opposite school buildings seen as Cambridge Street is passed, and the detour of a block is well worth taking. Continue on Chestnut Street or 

Broad Street to Boston Street, and follow marked route through part of Peabody to Lynnfield and rejoin Newburyport Turnpike, thus passing through an interesting district. 

The route may be continued beyond Newburyport by crossing bridge into Salisbury and westerly to

Amesbury, for years the home of many Quaker families. The inhabitants are largely engaged in making automobile bodies and lamps, continuing former carriage manufacture. On Friend Street, off Main 

Street, Whittier lived from 1836 to 1892. The poet's furnishings within the house have been retained. Four hundred and thirty-two of his poems were written here. Whittier was buried in the family lot, Union Cemetery, behind the old Macy house, built before 1654. 

Merrimac (3 m.) is passed through before reaching Whittier's birthplace, a modest, white farmhouse, on the right-hand side of the road. 

Haverhill (7.5 m.), founded in 1640, at the head of navigation of the 

Merrimack River, is 18 miles from the ocean. In those days there were quantities of shad and salmon in the river. The farmers used the fish for fertilizer. Twice Haverhill suffered from Indian raids. In 1697 Hannah 

Dustin was carried off with her week-old baby. Near the site of Concord, New Hampshire, Mrs. Dustin succeeded in slaying her captors and escaped. In 1708 Indians and French attacked the town and massacred sixteen inhabitants.

Haverhill's specialty is the production of fine footwear, which began in 1795. Today two hundred firms are engaged in the shoe industry. The 

Spiller or Hazen Garrison House, dating from about 1680, is said to be the first building used as a shoe factory. 

Across the river is Bradford, now a residential section of Haverhill, home of Bradford Academy, founded 1803, oldest seminary in the United

States for the education of young women. 

Take Highway 110 to Lawrence (9.3 m.), eighty-five years ago a settlement of a hundred people, now the center of America's woolen manufacture. In 1844 the Essex Company was formed to throw a dam across the Merrimack. This dam, a thousand feet across, produces 

12,000 horse power, distributed to the mill by canals on either bank. The 

Wood Mill, manufacturing serges and suitings, is the largest single mill

plant in the world, covering twenty-nine acres of floor space. The Everett 

Mill, now turned to other uses, was the biggest cotton mill in this country. 

The Pacific Mills are said to be the largest print works in the world. 

The Champion-International Company is one of the largest plants in the world producing coated paper, used by such publications as Scribner's, Country Life, and the National Geographic Magazine. 

From Lawrence there is a choice of two routes back to Boston. The shorter passes through Andover, Reading, and Medford. The longer continues along the river to Lowell, another great textile center, and then returns to Boston.

To take the shorter route, turn left on Broadway, Lawrence, and follow Highway 28 through Shawsheen (part of Andover, developed originally as a model village for employees of the Shawsheen Mill, one of the last big mills to be built for the manufacture of worsted cloth). 

Andover (1.3 m.), the seat of Phillips Academy, established in 1778 by 

Samuel Phillips, John Phillips, and Samuel Phillips, Jr. Six hundred and fifty boys are in attendance. The campus is probably the most beautiful of any secondary school campus in the country. Abbot Academy, half a mile from Phillips Academy, is the first girls' school incorporated in America (1829). 

At 147 Main Street on the right, going from Andover Square toward 

Phillips Academy, S. F. Smith wrote the hymn "America " while a student at Andover Theological Seminary. To the left, down a short street, we catch a glimpse of a stone house where Harriet Beecher Stowe wrote her great novel "Dred." The white colonial mansion on the right, opposite the campus, was the home of Elizabeth Stuart Phelps. 

Continue along Highway 28, the old Andover Turnpike, through

Reading and Stoneham, to Medford (16 m.), founded in 1629, famous for shipbuilding and rum-distilling. The Royall House, to the right of Main 

Street was the "grandest in North America" when built, in 1737. On 

Ship Street is the early brick dwelling built by Peter Tufts, long erroneously known as the Craddock House. Turn left just beyond the bridge at Medford Square and enter Boston by the boulevard. 

If the longer route to Boston is chosen, follow the north bank of the Merrimack on Highway 110 to Lowell (10 m.). In 1822 some Bostonians organized the Merrimack Manufacturing Company and erected a factory here. The town was named after Francis Cabot Lowell, whose success in developing the power loom and other cotton machinery made profitable the establishment of the mills. Today Lowell is one of the great diversified manufacturing cities of New England. There are 6 miles of distributing canals, which carry 30,000 developed horse power to the mills. 

Its industries are cotton goods, woolen and worsted goods, foundry and machine-shop products, boots and shoes, and proprietary medicines. 

From Lowell the shortest route back to Boston is by Highway 3, a distance of 25.4 miles.

CONVENTION TOURS MONTREAL AND QUEBEC 

American Legion members and their guests are offered reduced rates from Boston to Montreal and return ($10) and to Quebec ($5.65 additional). Tickets will be good on regular night trains from North Station 

October 9, day and night trains October 10 and 11, returning on either day or night trains October 12 and 13.

THE BERKSHIRES 

The Boston and Maine Railroad will sell round-trip tickets to North

Adams October 5 and 12 ($3), through the hill section of Massachusetts, famous for its autumnal foliage. Passengers wishing to leave train at 

Greenfield and proceed over the scenic and historic Mohawk Trail will find motor coaches at Greenfield. The additional charge for this service is $1. Special trains leave North Station 8.35 A.M.; leave North Adams

FLOAT PAGEANT PARADE 

The October 5 event will be a historical pageant-parade of which the main feature will be thirty-nine specially constructed floats portraying significant events and circumstances in the three centuries of city and state. The people on the floats will enact many of the events, thus making the whole production a pageant on wheels." A list of the floats, furnishing an outline sketch of America's history in Massachusetts, follows:

THE AMERICAN LEGION NATIONAL CONVENTION PARADE 

Boston, October 7, 1930, 10 A.M.

The most interesting and outstanding feature of each National Convention is the official parade of Legionnaires, and in this connection the National Commander is authorized to issue such regulations as may be necessary to provide that this parade shall always be of such a nature as to be truly in keeping with the aims and dignity of the American Legion, bearing in mind that in this parade not only the living Legionnaires march before the public, but with them, marching shoulder to shoulder and side by side, go the invisible spirits of our comrades who have "gone West."

The following appointments and regulations have been approved by the 

National Organization:

Commanding Officers and their staffs shall execute both the Hand 

Salute and Eyes Right (or Left) on passing the reviewing officials. Their organizations shall execute only Eyes Right (or Left).

Bands and Drum Corps will be advised by Parade Aides, as well as in Parade Orders, when to start playing prior to passing the Reviewing Stands, and also when to stop playing before reaching the former point. 

DISMISSAL 

Shortly after passing the Grand Marshal's Reviewing Stand, dislocation will be effected, as will be outlined in Parade Orders, over different routes. Parade Aides attached to each Department will also designate the route and the point of dismissal which the tail of the column must clear.

FORTY AND EIGHT PARADE Monday, October 6, 7.30 P.M. 

The parade will form in five divisions on Arlington, Berkeley, Clarendon, and Dartmouth Streets, between Commonwealth Avenue and 

Beacon Street. 

The First and Second Divisions will form on Dartmouth Street, facing toward Beacon Street and marching out on Beacon Street. 

The Third Division will form on Clarendon Street, facing toward Beacon 

Street and marching out on Beacon Street.

The Fourth Division will form on Berkeley Street, facing toward Beacon 

Street and marching out on Beacon Street. 

The Fifth Division will form on Arlington Street, facing toward Beacon 

Street and marching out on Beacon Street. 

All Floats, Box Cars, Trains, and Features will form on Marlborough

Street, nearest their respective divisions, facing toward Arlington Street and marching with their own divisions. 

The route of the parade will be Beacon Street to Park, to Tremont, to Stuart, to Columbus Avenue, to Massachusetts Avenue, to St. Botolph 

Street, and disbanding into the Arena for the National Wreck. 

All Voyageurs will have to show their 1930 cards for admission to the National Wreck. No groups will be allowed in as a unit. Formations into the Arena will be single file. There will be no need for a crush, as there are sixteen doors for admittance. Molestation of the candidates will not be permitted.

All bands and drum corps will be allowed to march with their own divisions. The placing in line will be under the direction of the marshals, who will endeavor to place these organizations in such a manner as to assist those units without music. The coöperation of these organizations will be appreciated by the committee.

Caution. Be careful in the handling of all illuminations. The others will consider your safety as you consider theirs. 

Parade forms at 7 P.M. and marches off sharp at 7.30 P.M.

Chief Marshal, Chef de Chemin de Fer E. Snapper Ingram. 

National Conducteur, Harry Freeman.

NOTE 

The chief merit of a guide book is that it brings the history of its subject to the present moment. Such has been the aim in the preparation of this little book. Yet it is something more than a guide to Boston: it is an historical itinerary, a progress from past to present. Its scope embraces, besides the municipality of Boston proper, the various communities which are comprehended in the term "Greater Boston"; historic places and literary shrines beyond these limits, as Salem, Plymouth, and Concord; the North Shore and the South Shore of Massachusetts

Bay. Care has been taken to provide the visitor with every possible aid to the convenient and comfortable exploration of the territory treated. 

The typographical arrangement, with the use of varied types to emphasize places, points, and objects, is designed to make the material available for quick reference. A helpful table of contents, the logical arrangement of the material, the headlines, and a complete alphabetical index contribute to the usefulness of the guide. In the mechanical execution the publishers have endeavored to present the book in a convenient and attractive format.

Other distinctive and superior features of this guide are: 

1. The material is authentic as it has been obtained by reference to original sources and documents. 

2. The diagrams and maps scattered through the pages are based on the latest information provided by the City Planning Board. The map of down-town Boston (pp. 4-5), engraved especially for this guide, has on its reverse pages an alphabetical and locational list of the principal streets and avenues, squares and historic burying grounds, parks, buildings, bridges, and wharves. 

3. The text is profusely illustrated. 

Strangers will find the section entitled "The Way about Town" (pp. vii-ix) particularly valuable. Visitors who have only two or three days to spend in Boston will find the section "Important Points of 

Interest" (pp. 147-151) helpful in arranging an itinerary. vi

INTRODUCTORY THE WAY ABOUT TOWN 

The stranger visiting Boston for the first time will find the city's reputation of being exceedingly intricate and tortuous to be deserved. But he may quickly get a general idea of the directions of the streets, and of the ways of reaching desired points, if he will grasp at the outset three important facts, as follows:

I. The well-worn term "The Hub" applies to down-town 

Boston in no mere fanciful sense. Roughly, the streets of this confusing Central District form a sort of wheel. The hub of the wheel, however, is not one fixed point, for the streets radiate from several squares. The map (pp. 4-5) shows at a glance that the figure of the wheel applies with sufficient exactness to warrant its use. In fact the stranger will save himself many steps

and much time by ascertaining at once the names and directions of a few main thoroughfares of the Central District, among them State Street, Milk Street, Washington Street, Tremont Street, Beacon Street, Summer Street, Hanover 

Street, and Atlantic Avenue.

II. The Back Bay District is arranged chiefly in the form of a rectangle, its eastern border united to the Central District described above at the Public Garden. 

The accompanying map indicates its general form and points out the principal connections with down-town Boston. vii

viii 

THE WAY ABOUT TOWN 

III. There are in Boston several important points of arrival or departure in which all routes center. The visitor cannot go far astray if he makes himself familiar with these few landmarks. The most essential are the following: 

Copley Square. Through this square, Boylston Street, running nearly east and west, is an important thoroughfare. Huntington Avenue, diverging to the southwest from Boylston Street at this square, is another artery. Stuart Street leaves Huntington Avenue one block southwest of the square and leads through the newly developed Park Square District to Washington Street. Dartmouth 

Street crosses the square running north and south. On the southern side of the square Trinity Place is a direct approach to the New York Central Trinity Place THE SOUTH STATION 

Station (one block) where all outgoing trains of this line stop; and at Huntington Avenue and Irvington Street (one block southwest of the square) is an approach to the Huntington Avenue Station of the same line, where all inward bound trains stop. 

Pedestrians may also reach both of these stations by steps leading down from opposite sides of the Dartmouth Street bridge over the New York Central tracks. Dartmouth Street also leads to the Back Bay 

Station of the New York,

New Haven & Hartford 

Railroad (one block and a half south), the stopping place for all trains in both directions. In or about Copley Square are grouped many important buildings, institutions, churches, and hotels.

Park Square, the wide opening south of Boylston Street, where the Public Garden and the Common are separated by Charles Street. Here, between the up-town and down-town retail shopping districts, the construction of new hotels and office buildings is an outstanding feature of Boston's recent development. Park Square is a terminus for interurban lines of motor coaches. 

The Intersection of Washington, Summer, and Winter streets, in the middle of the down-town business quarter. Washington Street is not only the great artery of retail traffic but it is the main highway of travel north and south through the older part of the city. Winter Street, but one block long, connects with Tremont 

Street at the Park Street Subway station; Summer Street is practically a continuation of it eastward to the South Station and the water. 

Park Street, also in the down-town business quarter. Here are the central stations of the Subway at the head of the Common, the most important point 

---

THE WAY ABOUT TOWN 

ix in the rapid-transit service of the city (see map, p. 38). At the head of the short street (a single block in length) is the State House; at its foot is the thoroughfare of Tremont Street, running north and south, from which cross streets at irregular intervals lead easterly to various parts of the general business districts. 

Scollay Square at the junction of the following streets, Tremont, Court,

Brattle, Hanover, Sudbury, Cambridge, and Howard; also of Cornhill and 

Pemberton Square. The Subway station at this point is a convergence of several lines of the rapid-transit system. The northern parts of the city are conveniently reached from Scollay Square.

The North Station, Causeway Street. This is occupied by the several divisions of the Boston & Maine Railroad system, whence trains are taken for points north, east, and west. A North Station development programme was begun in 1927 which is making this railroad terminal the center of a large number of major projects for the construction of public buildings and for the widening of adjacent thoroughfares.

The South Station, Dewey Square, junction of Summer Street and Atlantic 

Avenue. Occupied by the New York, New Haven & Hartford and the New York 

Central Railroads, whence trains are taken for the south and west. 

General Information. Details of routes of the many and various trolley and motor-bus lines in the city, and connections with other lines, are issued by the Boston Elevated Railway Company. The several railroad companies also furnish elaborate information in illustrated folders and other forms as to points of interest in New England along their lines reached from Boston. These can be obtained by the visitor at the downtown Consolidated Ticket Offices, 67 Franklin Street. At the railroad stations are Information Bureaus, at which the stranger should freely apply for any directions desired.

BOSTON: A GUIDE BOOK 

I. MODERN BOSTON HISTORICAL SKETCH 

HE town of Boston was founded in 1630 by English colonists sent out by the "Governor and Company of the Massachusetts Bay in New England," under the lead of John Winthrop, the second governor of the Bay Colony, who arrived at Salem in June of that year with the charter of 1629. It originated in an order passed by the Court of Assistants sitting in the "Governor's House" in Charlestown, on the opposite side of the Charles River, first selected as their place of settlement. This order was adopted September 17 (7 O. S.) and established three towns at once by the simple dictum, "that Trimountane shalbe called Boston; Mattapan, Dorchester; & ye towne vpon Charles Ryver, Waterton." "Trimountane" consisted of a peninsula with three hills, the highest (the present Beacon Hill), as seen from Charlestown, presenting three distinct peaks. Hence this name, given it by the colonists from Endicott's company at Salem, who had preceded the Winthrop colonists in the Charlestown settlement. The Indian name was "Shawmutt," or "Shaumut," which signified, according to some authorities, "Living Waters," but according to others, "Where there is going by boat," or "Near the neck." The name of Boston was selected in recognition of the chief men of the company, who had come from Boston in England, and particularly Isaac Johnson, "the greatest furtherer of the Colony," who died at Charlestown on the day of the naming. The peninsula was chosen for the chief settlement primarily because of its springs, the colonists at Charlestown suffering disastrously from the use of brackish water. The Reverend William 

Blaxton, the pioneer white settler on the peninsula (coming about 1625), then living alone in his cottage on the highest hill slope, "came and acquainted the governor of an excellent spring there, withal inviting him and soliciting him thither.” 

The three-hilled peninsula originally contained only about 783 acres, cut into by deep coves, estuaries, inlets, and creeks. It faced the harbor, at the west end of Massachusetts Bay, into which empty the Charles and Mystic rivers. It was pear-shaped, a little more than a mile wide at its broadest, and less than three miles long, the stem, or neck, connecting it with the mainland (at what became Roxbury) a mile in length, and so low and narrow that parts were not infrequently overflowed by the tides. By the reclamation of the broad marshes and flats from time to time, and the filling of the great coves, the original area of

BOSTON PROPER 

783 acres has been expanded to 1801 acres; and where it was the narrowest it is now the widest. Additional territory has been acquired by the development of East Boston and South Boston, and by the annexation of adjoining cities and towns. Thus the area of the city has become more than thirty times as large as that of the peninsula on which the town was built. Its bounds now embrace 

30,598 acres, or 47.81 square miles. Its extreme length, from north to south, CHARLES RIVER NEW BOSTON OLD BOSTON PUBLIC GARDEN BOSTON COMMON WASHINGTON HARBOR NEW BOSTON 

www 

POINT ATLANTIS CHANNEL 

FORT 

OLD AND NEW BOSTON 

is thirteen miles, and its extreme breadth, from east to west, nine miles. While the Colonial town was confined to the little peninsula, its jurisdiction at first extended over a large territory, which embraced the present cities and towns of Chelsea and Revere on the north, and Brookline, Quincy, Braintree, and Randolph on the west and south. So there was quite a respectable "Greater 

Boston" in those old first days. The metropolitan proportions continued till 1640 and were not entirely reduced to the limits of the peninsula and certain harbor islands till 1739. 

East Boston is comprised in two harbor islands: Noddle's Island, which was " layd to Boston" in 1637, and Breed's (earlier Hog) Island, annexed in 1635. South Boston was formerly Dorchester Neck, a part of the town of Dorchester, annexed in 

1804. The city of Roxbury (named as a town October 8, 1630) was annexed in 1868; the town of Dorchester (named in 1630 in the order naming 

Boston), in 1870; the city of Charlestown (founded as a town July 4, 1629), the town of Brighton (incorporated 1807), and the town of West Roxbury (incorporated 1851), by one act, in 1874; and the town of Hyde Park (incorporated 

1868), in 1912. These annexed municipalities retain their names with the term "District" added to each. Boston remained under town government, with a board of selectmen, till 1822. It was incorporated a city, February 23 of that year, after several ineffectual attempts to change the system. 

BOSTON PROPER 

The term " Boston Proper" is customarily used to designate the original city exclusive of the annexed parts; but for the purposes of this Guide we comprehend in the term the entire municipality, as distinguished from the allied cities and towns, closely identified with it in business and

SECTIONS OF THE CITY 

3 social relations, but yet independent political corporations. Together with the municipality these allied cities and towns constitute what is colloquially known as Greater Boston. This metropolitan community is officially recognized at present only in one state District Commission with three Divisions, ― Metropolitan Parks, Metropolitan Water, and Metropolitan Sewerage, and in part in the Boston Postal District established by the Post Office Department. The Metropolitan Parks 

Division includes Boston and thirty-seven cities and towns within a radius of fifteen miles from the City Hall, having a combined population approximating 2,000,000. The Metropolitan Water Division includes twenty cities and towns; the Metropolitan Sewerage Division, twentyeight; and the Boston Postal District, twenty-three. The "Boston 

Basin," however, is regarded as constituting the true bounds of "Greater Boston." This includes a territory of some fifteen miles in width, lying between the bay on the east, the Blue Hills on the south, and the ridges of the Wellington Hills, sweeping from Waltham on the west round toward Cape Ann on the north. It embraces forty cities and towns. The population of Boston alone approximates 895,000. 

The present city is divided by long-established custom into several distinct sections. These are:

The Central District, or General Business Quarter The North End The West End The South End The Back Bay Quarter 

The Brighton District, on the northwest

The Roxbury District, on the south 

The West Roxbury District, on the southwest

The Dorchester District, on the southeast The Charlestown District, on the north The Hyde Park District, on the south East Boston on its two islands, on the northeast South Boston projecting into the harbor, on the east The Business Quarters now occupy not only the Central District but most of the North End and parts of the West End and of the South End, and penetrate even the Back Bay Quarter, laid out in comparatively modern times (1860-1886), where the bay had been, as the fairest residential quarter of the city and the place for its finest architectural monuments.

BOSTON PROPER 1. THE CENTRAL DISTRICT 

The Central District (see the map following) is of first interest to the visitor, for here are most of the older historic landmarks. This small quarter of the present city, together with the North End, embraces that part of the original peninsula to which the historic town - Colonial, Provincial, and Revolutionary Boston was practically confined. The town of 1630 was begun along the irregular water front, the principal houses being placed round about the upper part of what is now State Street, modern Boston's financial center, and on or near the neighboring Dock Square, back of the present Faneuil Hall, where was the first Town Dock, occupying nearly all of the present North Market Street, in the "Great 

Cove." The square originally at the head of State Street (first Market, then King Street), in the middle of which now stands the Old State House, was the first center of town life. At about this point, accordingly, our explorations naturally begin. 

Head of State Street and the Old State 

House. Our starting place is the square at the head of State Street, which the Old State House faces. This itself is one of the most notable historic spots in Boston. For the first quartercentury of Colony life the entire square, including the space occupied by the Old State House, was the public market place. Thursday was market day, — the day also of the "Thursday Lecture" by the ministers. As early as 1648 semiannual fairs were held here in June and October, each holding a market for two or three days. Here were first inflicted the drastic punishments of offenders against the rigorous laws, and here unorthodox literature was burned. 

The Stocks, the Whipping Post, and the Pillory were earliest placed here. When the town was a half-century old, a Cage for the confinement and exposure of violators of the rigid Sunday laws was added to these penal instruments. In the Revolutionary period the Stocks stood near the northeast corner of the Old State House, with the Whipping Post close by; and the Pillory when used was set in the middle of the square
